===== sec_gram_space.tex =====
\section{Mode Gram Space and Centred Reconstruction}%
\subsection{The mode Gram kernel and Hilbert completion}
\begin{theorem}[The mode Gram kernel]%
\label{thm:mode-gram-kernel}
For every $\varepsilon,\eta\in\RR$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mode-gram-kernel}
\langle \Psi_\varepsilon,\Psi_\eta\rangle_{L^2(\omega)}
=\frac{2}{4+(\varepsilon-\eta)^2}.
\end{equation}
Equivalently,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mode-gram-RR}
\int_{\RR}R_\varepsilon(y)R_\eta(y)\,\omega(\dd y)
=\frac{\varepsilon^2+\eta^2+4}{(\varepsilon-\eta)^2+4}.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{corollary}[Closed formulas for quadratic mode integrals]%
\label{cor:mode-quadratic-integrals}
For $|\varepsilon|<1$ one has the $L^2(\omega)$ expansion
\[
\Psi_\varepsilon
=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}u_n\,\varepsilon^{n-1}.
\]
Consequently, for every $p,q\ge 1$,
\[
\langle u_{2p},u_{2q}\rangle_{L^2(\omega)}
=(-1)^{p+q}2^{-(2p+2q-1)}
\binom{2p+2q-2}{2p-1},
\]
for every $p,q\ge 0$,
\[
\langle u_{2p+1},u_{2q+1}\rangle_{L^2(\omega)}
=(-1)^{p+q}2^{-(2p+2q+1)}
\binom{2p+2q}{2p},
\]
and
\[
\langle u_{2p},u_{2q+1}\rangle_{L^2(\omega)}=0.
\]
\end{corollary}

\begin{lemma}[Poisson-transform completeness of the secant modes]%
\label{lem:poisson-injectivity-weighted}
Let
\[
\omega(\dd y)=\frac{\dd y}{\pi(1+y^2)}
\]
and let \(f\in L^2(\omega)\) satisfy
\[
\int_{\RR}f(y)\,\omega(\dd y)=0 .
\]
If
\[
\langle f,\Psi_\varepsilon\rangle_{L^2(\omega)}=0
\qquad(\varepsilon\in\RR),
\]
then \(f=0\) in \(L^2(\omega)\).  Equivalently, the algebraic span of
\(\{\Psi_\varepsilon:\varepsilon\in\RR\}\) is dense in \(L^2_0(\omega)\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{theorem}[The mode space and its RKHS completion]%
\label{thm:mode-space-rkhs}
Let
\[
L^2_0(\omega):=
\left\{
f\in L^2(\omega):\ \int_{\RR}f(y)\,\omega(\dd y)=0
\right\},
\]
and let $\mathcal H_K$ be the reproducing-kernel Hilbert space generated
by the kernel
\[
K(a,b):=\frac{2}{4+(a-b)^2}.
\]
Set
\[
\mathcal S_0:=\operatorname{span}\{\Psi_\varepsilon:\varepsilon\in\RR\},
\qquad
\mathcal S:=\overline{\mathcal S_0}^{\,L^2(\omega)}
\subset L^2_0(\omega),
\]
Then
\[
\mathcal S=L^2_0(\omega).
\]
Equivalently, the algebraic span
$\mathcal S_0$ is dense in~$L^2_0(\omega)$.
The density assertion rests on the isolated Poisson-transform completeness
lemma immediately above: orthogonality to all secant kernels, together with
the zero-mean condition, says that the Poisson transform \(P_1*f\) vanishes
on the line.  Fourier injectivity of the strictly positive Poisson multiplier
then forces \(f=0\).
Moreover, the linear assignment
\[
\mathcal U_0:\mathcal S_0\longrightarrow \mathcal H_K,
\qquad
\mathcal U_0(\Psi_\varepsilon):=K(\cdot,\varepsilon)
\qquad(\varepsilon\in\RR)
\]
is an isometry and extends uniquely to a unitary operator
\[
\mathcal U:L^2_0(\omega)\longrightarrow \mathcal H_K.
\]
\end{theorem}

\subsection{Centred reconstruction from Poisson data}
\begin{proposition}[Centred Poisson reconstruction by Laplace uniqueness]%
\label{prop:poisson-central-two-channel}
Let $\nu$ be a probability measure on~$\RR$ with finite mean~$\bar{\gamma}$.
Let $\nu_c$ denote the law of the centred variable
$X_c:=\gamma-\bar{\gamma}$.  Define
\[
h_t(x):=\int_{\RR}P_t(x-\gamma)\dd\nu(\gamma),
\]
the central observation functions
\[
A(t):=\pi\,h_t(\bar{\gamma}),
\qquad
B(t):=\pi\,\partial_x h_t(x)\big|_{x=\bar{\gamma}}
\qquad(t>0).
\]
\[
H(t):=\int_t^\infty B(s)\dd s
\qquad(t>0),
\]
and let
\[
\phi_{\nu_c}(r):=\int_{\RR}e^{irX_c}\dd\nu(\gamma),
\qquad r\in\RR,
\]
be the centred characteristic function.  Then for every $t>0$ one has
\begin{equation}\label{eq:poisson-laplace-reconstruction}
A(t)+\ii H(t)
=\int_0^\infty e^{-tr}\phi_{\nu_c}(r)\dd r.
\end{equation}
Consequently, the pair $(A,B)$ determines the centred law~$\nu_c$
uniquely.  Equivalently, the triple $(\bar{\gamma},A,B)$ determines the
original measure~$\nu$ uniquely.
\end{proposition}

\begin{corollary}[Symmetric channel]%
\label{cor:poisson-symmetrization}
Under the hypotheses of Proposition~\ref{prop:poisson-central-two-channel}, the
function $A$ determines the symmetrization of the centred law~$\nu_c$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{corollary}[Symmetric case from the odd channel]%
\label{cor:poisson-symmetric-case}
Under the hypotheses of Proposition~\ref{prop:poisson-central-two-channel}, the
centred law~$\nu_c$ is symmetric if and only if $B\equiv 0$.  In that case
$A$ alone determines~$\nu_c$, and the pair $(\bar{\gamma},A)$ determines the
original law~$\nu$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{theorem}[Observation channels as evaluation functionals]%
\label{thm:poisson-channel-rkhs}
Let $\nu$ be a probability measure on~$\RR$ with finite mean~$\bar{\gamma}$.
For $t>0$ define the centred fluctuation profile
\[
\mathfrak d_t(y):=
\frac{h_t(\bar{\gamma}+ty)}{g_t(\bar{\gamma}+ty)}-1,
\qquad y\in\RR.
\]
Then $\mathfrak d_t\in L^2_0(\omega)$ and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:delta-t-secant}
\mathfrak d_t(y)
=\frac{1}{t}\int_{\RR}X_c\,\Psi_{X_c/t}(y)\,\dd\nu(\gamma).
\end{equation}
Consequently,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:delta-t-rkhs}
(\mathcal U\mathfrak d_t)(a)
=\frac{1}{t}\int_{\RR}X_c\,K(a,X_c/t)\,\dd\nu(\gamma),
\qquad a\in\RR.
\end{equation}
Moreover,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:delta-t-origin}
\mathfrak d_t(0)=tA(t)-1,
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:delta-t-rkhs-origin}
(\mathcal U\mathfrak d_t)(0)=2t\,H(2t)
=2t\int_{2t}^{\infty}B(s)\,\dd s.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{remark}
The previous theorem recovers the centred Cauchy transform on the
imaginary axis:
\[
G_{\nu_c}(\ii t)
=\int_{\RR}\frac{1}{\ii t-X_c}\dd\nu(\gamma)
=-H(t)-\ii A(t)
\qquad(t>0).
\]
Thus the Laplace-transform formulation strengthens the earlier
Cauchy-transform identity while also isolating the loss of the translation
parameter.  In particular, the proof of
Proposition~\ref{prop:poisson-central-two-channel} is better viewed as a
Laplace-uniqueness plus characteristic-function-inversion argument than
as a Stieltjes inversion argument.
\end{remark}

===== sec_strip_00_poisson_image.tex =====
\section{The Strip RKHS and Lattice Consequences}%
\subsection{A Poisson image realization of the mode space}
\begin{proposition}[The mode Gram space as a Poisson image space]%
\label{prop:gram-space-poisson-image}
Let
\[
K(a,b):=\frac{2}{4+(a-b)^2}=\pi\,P_2(a-b),
\qquad a,b\in\RR,
\]
and let $\mathcal H_K$ be the reproducing-kernel Hilbert space generated
by~$K$.  Define
\[
\mathcal X
:=
\left\{
f\in L^2(\RR):
\int_{\RR}e^{2|\xi|}\abs{\widehat f(\xi)}^2\dd\xi<\infty
\right\},
\]
with norm
\[
\|f\|_{\mathcal X}^2
:=\frac{1}{2\pi^2}\int_{\RR}e^{2|\xi|}\abs{\widehat f(\xi)}^2\dd\xi.
\]
Then $\mathcal H_K=\mathcal X$ isometrically.  Equivalently,
\[
\mathcal H_K=\sqrt{\pi}\,T_1(L^2(\RR)),
\qquad
\|\sqrt{\pi}\,T_1 g\|_{\mathcal H_K}=\|g\|_{L^2(\RR)}
\quad(g\in L^2(\RR)).
\]
Every $f\in\mathcal H_K$ admits a unique holomorphic extension to the
strip
\[
\mathbb S:=\{z\in\CC:\ |\Im z|<1\},
\]
given by
\[
f(z)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\RR}\widehat f(\xi)e^{\ii z\xi}\dd\xi,
\qquad z\in\mathbb S,
\]
and satisfying the sharp pointwise estimate
\[
|f(x+\ii y)|\le \frac{1}{\sqrt{2(1-y^2)}}\,\|f\|_{\mathcal H_K}
\qquad(x\in\RR,\ |y|<1).
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:poisson-image-framework}
The identity $K(a,b) = \pi P_2(a-b)$ means that the Gram kernel is,
up to the factor~$\pi$, the Poisson kernel at time~$2$.  This explains
why the RKHS is a Poisson image space: the kernel itself arises from the
semigroup.  The holomorphic extension and pointwise estimate above are
the standard $H^2$ Poisson-boundary statements transported to a
translation-invariant strip model.
\end{remark}

===== sec_strip_20_symbol_sampling.tex =====
\subsection{Imported symbol criterion and the Poisson integer-orbit symbol}%
\begin{lemma}[Classical imported principal-symbol sampling criterion]%
\label{lem:imported-principal-symbol-sampling}
This is a background result in the principal shift-invariant-space and
RKHS sampling literature, used here in the form of
\cite{deBoorDeVoreRon1994,RonShen1995,Bownik2000,AldroubiGrochenig2001,NashedWalter1991,NashedSun2010,ChengJiangSun2016}.
The density theorems
\cite{Beurling1989,Landau1967,Seip1992,FuhrGrochenigHaimiKlotzRomero2018}
motivate the terminology and comparison class; the proof below uses only the
displayed positive-symbol hypothesis.
Let $\mathcal H$ be a Hilbert space and let
$(\varphi_n)_{n\in\ZZ}$ be a family whose Gram matrix is Toeplitz,
\[
\langle\varphi_m,\varphi_n\rangle_{\mathcal H}=a_{m-n},
\qquad a\in\ell^1(\ZZ).
\]
Put
\[
\sigma(\theta):=\sum_{k\in\ZZ}a_ke^{-\ii k\theta},
\qquad -\pi\le\theta\le\pi,
\]
and suppose that $0<A\le\sigma(\theta)\le B<\infty$ a.e.  Then the
synthesis map
\[
C:c_{00}(\ZZ)\to\mathcal H,
\qquad Cc:=\sum_{n\in\ZZ}c_n\varphi_n,
\]
extends to a topological isomorphism from $\ell^2(\ZZ)$ onto the closed
orbit space $V:=\overline{\operatorname{span}}\{\varphi_n:n\in\ZZ\}$, with
\[
A\|c\|_{\ell^2}^2\le\|Cc\|_{\mathcal H}^2
\le B\|c\|_{\ell^2}^2.
\]
Let $G$ be the Toeplitz Gram operator on $\ell^2(\ZZ)$ with matrix
$(a_{m-n})_{m,n\in\ZZ}$.  The discrete Fourier transform diagonalizes
$G$ as multiplication by~$\sigma$, and $G^{-1}$ is multiplication by
$\sigma^{-1}$.  If, in addition, a bounded restriction map
$R:V\to\ell^2(\ZZ)$ satisfies $R(Cc)=Gc$ for all finitely supported $c$,
then $R$ is an isomorphism.  For every datum $\alpha\in\ell^2(\ZZ)$ the
unique interpolant $R^{-1}\alpha$ satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:imported-reciprocal-symbol-norm}
\|R^{-1}\alpha\|_{\mathcal H}^2
=\frac1{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}
\frac{|\widetilde\alpha(\theta)|^2}{\sigma(\theta)}\dd\theta .
\end{equation}
The cardinal element $L:=R^{-1}\delta_0$ is synthesized by
$d=G^{-1}\delta_0$, equivalently $\widetilde d=\sigma^{-1}$ a.e.
Thus the statement supplies the functional-analytic passage from a positive
principal symbol to Riesz bounds, bounded interpolation, and the
reciprocal-symbol norm.  In the Poisson setting,
Proposition~\ref{prop:poisson-lattice-symbol} and
Theorem~\ref{thm:poisson-channel-rkhs} verify these displayed hypotheses for
the strip kernel and observation profiles
\cite{deBoorDeVoreRon1994,RonShen1995,AldroubiGrochenig2001}.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proposition}[Explicit Poisson integer-orbit symbol]%
\label{prop:poisson-lattice-symbol}
Let
\[
K(a,b):=\frac{2}{4+(a-b)^2},
\qquad
\sigma(\theta):=\sum_{n\in\ZZ}K(n,0)e^{-\ii n\theta}
=\sum_{n\in\ZZ}\frac{2}{4+n^2}e^{-\ii n\theta}.
\]
Then $\sigma$ is continuous and $2\pi$-periodic, and for
$\theta\in[-\pi,\pi]$ one has
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lattice-symbol}
\sigma(\theta)
=\frac{\pi\cosh(2(\pi-|\theta|))}{\sinh(2\pi)}.
\end{equation}
Consequently the sharp lattice-symbol bounds are
\[
A_{\ZZ}:=\frac{\pi}{\sinh(2\pi)}
\le
\sigma(\theta)
\le
\pi\coth(2\pi)=:B_{\ZZ}
\qquad(\theta\in[-\pi,\pi]).
\]
Moreover, for every finitely supported scalar sequence
$c=(c_n)_{n\in\ZZ}$, with
\[
\widetilde c(\theta):=\sum_{n\in\ZZ}c_ne^{-\ii n\theta},
\]
one has the exact Gram identity
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lattice-symbol-form}
\sum_{m,n\in\ZZ}c_m\overline{c_n}\,K(m,n)
=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}
\sigma(\theta)|\widetilde c(\theta)|^2\dd\theta .
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

===== sec_strip_30_cardinal_observation.tex =====
\subsection{Poisson cardinal projection and observation-channel samples}%
\begin{corollary}[Explicit lattice cardinal kernel and norm]%
\label{cor:poisson-cardinal-reconstruction}
With the Poisson symbol consequences recorded immediately after
Proposition~\ref{prop:poisson-lattice-symbol}, let
\[
L:=R_{\ZZ}^{-1}\delta_0\in\mathcal H_K(\ZZ).
\]
Here $\mathcal H_K(\ZZ)$ is the closed span of the kernel sections
$\{K(\cdot,n):n\in\ZZ\}$.
Then $L(n)=\delta_{0n}$ for every $n\in\ZZ$.  For every
$\alpha=(\alpha_n)_{n\in\ZZ}\in\ell^2(\ZZ)$, the series
\[
F_\alpha(z):=\sum_{n\in\ZZ}\alpha_nL(z-n)
\]
converges in $\mathcal H_K(\ZZ)$ and uniformly on compact subsets of the
strip $\mathbb S$, and it is the unique function in $\mathcal H_K(\ZZ)$
satisfying
\[
F_\alpha(n)=\alpha_n
\qquad(n\in\ZZ).
\]
If
\[
\widetilde\alpha(\theta):=\sum_{n\in\ZZ}\alpha_ne^{-\ii n\theta},
\qquad \theta\in[-\pi,\pi],
\]
then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cardinal-norm-formula}
\|F_\alpha\|_{\mathcal H_K}^2
=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}
\frac{|\widetilde\alpha(\theta)|^2}{\sigma(\theta)}\dd\theta,
\end{equation}
where $\sigma$ is the lattice symbol from \eqref{eq:lattice-symbol}.  If
$d\in\ell^2(\ZZ)$ is the unique sequence satisfying
\[
\widetilde d(\theta)=\sigma(\theta)^{-1}
\qquad\text{for a.e. }\theta\in[-\pi,\pi],
\]
then
\[
L(z)=\sum_{n\in\ZZ}d_nK(z,n)
\]
in $\mathcal H_K$, and for almost every $\theta\in[-\pi,\pi]$ and every
$m\in\ZZ$ one has
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cardinal-kernel-fourier}
\widehat L(\theta+2\pi m)
=\frac{\pi e^{-2|\theta+2\pi m|}}{\sigma(\theta)}.
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}

\begin{observationprofilelemma}[Observation profile determines the centred law]%
\label{lem:observation-profile-determines-law}
Write \(X_c:=\gamma-\bar{\gamma}\).  Let \(\kappa_t\) be the distribution of
\(X_c/t\), and define the finite signed
measure
\[
\lambda_t(\dd s):=s\,\kappa_t(\dd s).
\]
For the Poisson observation channel in
Theorem~\ref{thm:poisson-observation-lattice-sampling},
\[
q_{F_t}(\xi)=e^{2|\xi|}\widehat F_t(\xi)=\pi\,\widehat{\lambda_t}(\xi)
\]
with the Fourier convention used throughout this section.  Hence \(q_{F_t}\)
determines \(\lambda_t\) by Fourier uniqueness.  It determines \(\kappa_t\) on
\(\RR\setminus\{0\}\) by
\[
\kappa_t(B)=\int_B s^{-1}\lambda_t(\dd s),
\qquad 0\notin\overline B,
\]
and then determines the atom at the origin from total mass:
\[
\kappa_t(\{0\})=1-\kappa_t(\RR\setminus\{0\}).
\]
Thus the full continuous profile \(q_{F_t}\) determines the centred law of
\(X_c/t\).  For the integer-orbit cardinal reconstruction formula to
represent this full channel, \(q_{F_t}\) must be \(2\pi\)-periodic,
equivalently \(\kappa_t\) must be supported on \(\ZZ\).
\end{observationprofilelemma}

\begin{theorem}[Integer-orbit projection of Poisson observation channels]%
\label{thm:poisson-observation-lattice-sampling}
\label{prop:poisson-law-lattice-periodicity}
Let $\nu$ be a probability measure on~$\RR$ with finite mean
$\bar{\gamma}$, let $t>0$, and let $\mathfrak d_t$ be the centred
fluctuation profile from Theorem~\ref{thm:poisson-channel-rkhs}.  Put
\[
  X_c:=\gamma-\bar{\gamma},
  \qquad
F_t:=\mathcal U\mathfrak d_t\in\mathcal H_K,
\]
and let $\Pi_{\ZZ}$ denote the orthogonal projection of $\mathcal H_K$ onto
$\mathcal H_K(\ZZ)$.  Define the integer-orbit observation samples
\[
\alpha_n(t):=F_t(n),\qquad n\in\ZZ .
\]
By Theorem~\ref{thm:poisson-channel-rkhs}, these samples are evaluations of
the centred Poisson observation profile against the secant modes at the
integer orbit.  The projection $\Pi_{\ZZ}$ is the lattice component of that
observation channel, with constants and norm formula determined by the exact
Poisson symbol \eqref{eq:lattice-symbol}.  The reconstruction of the lattice
component uses the standard principal-symbol projection mechanism recorded in
Lemma~\ref{lem:imported-principal-symbol-sampling}.  Full ambient recovery is
equivalent to the law-side condition
\[
\nu\bigl(\{\gamma\in\RR:X_c\in t\ZZ\}\bigr)=1;
\]
the theorem lists the equivalent RKHS and periodicity formulations below.
Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:poisson-observation-integer-samples}
\alpha_n(t)
=\langle \mathfrak d_t,\Psi_n\rangle_{L^2(\omega)}
=\frac1t\int_{\RR}
(\gamma-\bar{\gamma})\,
K\!\left(n,\frac{\gamma-\bar{\gamma}}{t}\right)\dd\nu(\gamma),
\qquad n\in\ZZ .
\end{equation}
Moreover $\alpha(t):=(\alpha_n(t))_{n\in\ZZ}\in\ell^2(\ZZ)$ and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:poisson-observation-lattice-stability}
A_{\ZZ}\|\Pi_{\ZZ}F_t\|_{\mathcal H_K}^2
\le
\sum_{n\in\ZZ}|\alpha_n(t)|^2
\le
B_{\ZZ}\|\Pi_{\ZZ}F_t\|_{\mathcal H_K}^2
\le
B_{\ZZ}\|\mathfrak d_t\|_{L^2(\omega)}^2.
\end{equation}
The cardinal reconstruction with these observable samples is the lattice
projection:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:poisson-observation-cardinal-projection}
\Pi_{\ZZ}F_t(z)
=\sum_{n\in\ZZ}\alpha_n(t)L(z-n)
\qquad(z\in\mathbb S),
\end{equation}
with convergence in $\mathcal H_K(\ZZ)$ and uniformly on compact subsets
of~$\mathbb S$, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:poisson-observation-cardinal-norm}
\|\Pi_{\ZZ}F_t\|_{\mathcal H_K}^2
=\frac1{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}
\frac{|\widetilde\alpha_t(\theta)|^2}{\sigma(\theta)}\dd\theta,
\qquad
\widetilde\alpha_t(\theta):=
\sum_{n\in\ZZ}\alpha_n(t)e^{-\ii n\theta}.
\end{equation}
Finally,
\[
F_t-\Pi_{\ZZ}F_t\in\mathcal H_K(\ZZ)^\perp
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
(F_t-\Pi_{\ZZ}F_t)(n)=0\quad(n\in\ZZ).
\]
Thus the integer samples reconstruct $\Pi_{\ZZ}F_t$ from the data
$(F_t(n))_{n\in\ZZ}$.  The next display gives the law-side criterion for this
projection to equal the full observation channel.
Define the law-side spectral profile
\begin{equation}\label{eq:poisson-law-side-profile}
Q_t(\xi)
:=\frac{\pi}{t}\int_{\RR}X_c
e^{-\ii X_c\xi/t}\,\dd\nu(\gamma),
\qquad \xi\in\RR .
\end{equation}
Then $Q_t$ is bounded and continuous, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:poisson-law-side-q-profile}
q_{F_t}(\xi):=e^{2|\xi|}\widehat F_t(\xi)=Q_t(\xi)
\qquad\text{for a.e. }\xi\in\RR .
\end{equation}
Consequently, the following are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item the integer-orbit cardinal reconstruction in
      \eqref{eq:poisson-observation-cardinal-projection} recovers the full
      Poisson observation channel $F_t$;
\item $F_t\in\mathcal H_K(\ZZ)$;
\item the law-side profile $Q_t$ is $2\pi$-periodic, equivalently
      \begin{equation}\label{eq:poisson-law-side-periodicity}
      \int_{\RR}X_c e^{-\ii X_c(\xi+2\pi)/t}\,\dd\nu(\gamma)
      =
      \int_{\RR}X_c e^{-\ii X_c\xi/t}\,\dd\nu(\gamma)
      \qquad(\xi\in\RR).
      \end{equation}
\item the centred law has no mass off the lattice $t\ZZ$, equivalently
      \begin{equation}\label{eq:poisson-law-side-lattice-support}
      \nu\bigl(\{\gamma\in\RR:\gamma-\bar{\gamma}\in t\ZZ\}\bigr)=1 .
      \end{equation}
\end{enumerate}
In this case the lattice norm of the full channel is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:poisson-law-side-lattice-norm}
\|F_t\|_{\mathcal H_K}^2
=\frac{1}{2\pi^2}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}
\Sigma_1(\xi)|Q_t(\xi)|^2\,\dd\xi
=\frac{1}{2\pi^3}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}
\sigma(\xi)|Q_t(\xi)|^2\,\dd\xi,
\end{equation}
where
\[
\Sigma_1(\xi):=\sum_{m\in\ZZ}e^{-2|\xi+2\pi m|}
=\frac{\sigma(\xi)}{\pi}.
\]
Equivalently, if $\phi_{\nu_c}(s):=\int_{\RR}e^{\ii sX_c}\dd\nu(\gamma)$
is the characteristic function of the centred law, then, with the Fourier
convention $\widehat f(\xi)=\int f(x)e^{-\ii x\xi}\dd x$,
\[
Q_t(\xi)=-\frac{\pi\ii}{t}\,\phi_{\nu_c}'(-\xi/t),
\]
so the full-recovery condition is precisely the $2\pi$-periodicity of this
signed first characteristic-function channel.  Negative frequencies are read
with the usual conjugate symmetry
$\phi_{\nu_c}(-s)=\overline{\phi_{\nu_c}(s)}$, and no symmetry of the law is
assumed.  Equivalently, the centred law is supported on the affine lattice
$\bar{\gamma}+t\ZZ$.
\end{theorem}

\ifdefined\JFAArticleMain
The periodized-exponential calculation proving this proposition is in
\path{sec_strip_20_symbol_sampling.tex}, proposition ``Explicit Poisson
integer-orbit symbol,'' as compiled by
\path{supplement_relocated_support.tex}.

\begin{proof}
Set $k(x):=K(x,0)=2/(4+x^2)$.  With the Fourier convention used in this
paper,
\[
\widehat k(\xi)=\pi e^{-2|\xi|}.
\]
The defining series for $\sigma$ is absolutely and uniformly convergent,
so $\sigma$ is continuous and $2\pi$-periodic.
The Poisson summation formula \cite[Chapter~III]{Stein1971} gives
\[
\sigma(\theta)=\sum_{m\in\ZZ}\widehat k(\theta+2\pi m)
=\pi\sum_{m\in\ZZ}e^{-2|\theta+2\pi m|}.
\]
For $0\le\theta\le\pi$ this geometric series equals
\[
\pi\left(e^{-2\theta}
+\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}e^{-2(\theta+2\pi j)}
+\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}e^{-2(2\pi j-\theta)}\right)
=\pi\,\frac{\cosh(2(\pi-\theta))}{\sinh(2\pi)}.
\]
The expression is even in~$\theta$, so \eqref{eq:lattice-symbol} follows
on $[-\pi,\pi]$, and periodicity gives the full symbol.
The minimum and maximum of the displayed formula occur respectively at
$|\theta|=\pi$ and $\theta=0$, which gives the sharp constants
$A_{\ZZ}$ and $B_{\ZZ}$.

Finally, since $K(m,n)=k(m-n)$, Parseval's identity for the discrete
Fourier transform gives
\[
\sum_{m,n\in\ZZ}c_m\overline{c_n}\,K(m,n)
=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}
\left(\sum_{\ell\in\ZZ}k(\ell)e^{-\ii\ell\theta}\right)
|\widetilde c(\theta)|^2\dd\theta,
\]
which is exactly \eqref{eq:lattice-symbol-form}.
\end{proof}

Define
\[
\mathcal H_K(\ZZ):=
\overline{\operatorname{span}}\{K(\cdot,n):n\in\ZZ\}
\subset\mathcal H_K,
\qquad
A_{\ZZ}:=\frac{\pi}{\sinh(2\pi)},\quad
B_{\ZZ}:=\pi\coth(2\pi).
\]
The Riesz bounds, restriction isomorphism, sampling inequality, and their
proof are in \path{sec_strip_20_symbol_sampling.tex}, paragraph

by \path{supplement_relocated_support.tex}.

\paragraph{Consequences of the imported criterion for the Poisson symbol.}
Define the closed shift-generated Hilbert subspaces
\[
\mathcal H_K(\ZZ)
:=
\overline{\operatorname{span}}\{K(\cdot,n):n\in\ZZ\}
\subset \mathcal H_K,
\]
\[
\mathcal S_{\ZZ}
:=
\overline{\operatorname{span}}\{\Psi_n:n\in\ZZ\}
\subset L^2_0(\omega).
\]
Thus $\mathcal H_K(\ZZ)$ and $\mathcal S_{\ZZ}$ are the Hilbert closures
of finite linear combinations of the corresponding kernel sections and
secants.
Set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lattice-sampling-constants}
A_{\ZZ}:=\frac{\pi}{\sinh(2\pi)},
\qquad
B_{\ZZ}:=\pi\coth(2\pi).
\end{equation}
We apply the imported principal-symbol criterion
Lemma~\ref{lem:imported-principal-symbol-sampling} once, with the Toeplitz
kernel and symbol computed in
Proposition~\ref{prop:poisson-lattice-symbol}.  This gives the following
standard Riesz and sampling consequences.  The passage from a positive
Toeplitz symbol to Riesz bases, restriction isomorphisms, and cardinal
interpolation is background shift-invariant/RKHS sampling theory; the
article-specific input is the closed Poisson symbol
$\sigma(\theta)=\pi\cosh(2(\pi-|\theta|))/\sinh(2\pi)$ and the induced
constants $A_{\ZZ},B_{\ZZ}$.  The families
$\{K(\cdot,n)\}_{n\in\ZZ}$ and $\{\Psi_n\}_{n\in\ZZ}$ are Riesz bases for
$\mathcal H_K(\ZZ)$ and $\mathcal S_{\ZZ}$, respectively.  More
precisely, for every
finitely supported scalar sequence $(c_n)_{n\in\ZZ}$ one has
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lattice-riesz-kernel}
A_{\ZZ}\sum_{n\in\ZZ}|c_n|^2
\le
\left\|
\sum_{n\in\ZZ}c_nK(\cdot,n)
\right\|_{\mathcal H_K}^2
\le
B_{\ZZ}\sum_{n\in\ZZ}|c_n|^2,
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lattice-riesz-secant}
A_{\ZZ}\sum_{n\in\ZZ}|c_n|^2
\le
\left\|
\sum_{n\in\ZZ}c_n\Psi_n
\right\|_{L^2(\omega)}^2
\le
B_{\ZZ}\sum_{n\in\ZZ}|c_n|^2.
\end{equation}
Consequently, the synthesis maps
\[
\mathcal C_K(c):=\sum_{n\in\ZZ}c_nK(\cdot,n),
\qquad
\mathcal C_S(c):=\sum_{n\in\ZZ}c_n\Psi_n,
\qquad c\in c_{00}(\ZZ),
\]
extend uniquely to topological isomorphisms
\[
\mathcal C_K:\ell^2(\ZZ)\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal H_K(\ZZ),
\qquad
\mathcal C_S:\ell^2(\ZZ)\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal S_{\ZZ}.
\]
If $F\in\mathcal H_K(\ZZ)$, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lattice-sampling-HK}
A_{\ZZ}\|F\|_{\mathcal H_K}^2
\le
\sum_{n\in\ZZ}|F(n)|^2
\le
B_{\ZZ}\|F\|_{\mathcal H_K}^2.
\end{equation}
Consequently, the restriction map
\[
R_{\ZZ}:\mathcal H_K(\ZZ)\to \ell^2(\ZZ),
\qquad
R_{\ZZ}F:=(F(n))_{n\in\ZZ},
\]
is an isomorphism of Hilbert spaces.  Equivalently, every
$\alpha\in\ell^2(\ZZ)$ admits a unique interpolant
$F_\alpha\in\mathcal H_K(\ZZ)$ satisfying
\[
F_\alpha(n)=\alpha_n
\qquad(n\in\ZZ).
\]

Indeed, the hypotheses of the imported criterion are verified as follows.
For finitely supported $c=(c_n)_{n\in\ZZ}$ set
\[
F_c:=\sum_{n\in\ZZ}c_nK(\cdot,n),
\qquad
\widetilde c(\theta):=\sum_{n\in\ZZ}c_ne^{-\ii n\theta},
\qquad
\theta\in[-\pi,\pi].
\]
By Theorem~\ref{thm:mode-gram-kernel},
\[
\|F_c\|_{\mathcal H_K}^2
=\sum_{m,n\in\ZZ}c_m\overline{c_n}\,K(m,n)
=\sum_{m,n\in\ZZ}c_m\overline{c_n}\,\frac{2}{4+(m-n)^2}.
\]
Thus \eqref{eq:lattice-symbol-form} from
Proposition~\ref{prop:poisson-lattice-symbol} gives
\[
\|F_c\|_{\mathcal H_K}^2
=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\sigma(\theta)
|\widetilde c(\theta)|^2\dd\theta.
\]
Combining the sharp symbol bounds from
Proposition~\ref{prop:poisson-lattice-symbol} with Parseval for
$\widetilde c$ proves \eqref{eq:lattice-riesz-kernel}.  Since
$\mathcal U\Psi_n=K(\cdot,n)$ by
Theorem~\ref{thm:mode-space-rkhs}, \eqref{eq:lattice-riesz-secant}
follows immediately.
The two inequalities show that $\mathcal C_K$ and $\mathcal C_S$
are bounded above and below on the dense subspace $c_{00}(\ZZ)$ of
$\ell^2(\ZZ)$.  Hence they extend uniquely to bounded topological
isomorphisms from $\ell^2(\ZZ)$ onto their closed ranges.  By definition
those closed ranges are precisely $\mathcal H_K(\ZZ)$ and
$\mathcal S_{\ZZ}$, so the stated Riesz-basis assertions follow.

Now let $G$ be the Toeplitz Gram operator on $\ell^2(\ZZ)$ associated
with the matrix $(K(m,n))_{m,n\in\ZZ}$.  For finitely supported $c$ one
has $R_{\ZZ}F_c=Gc$, so
\[
\sum_{n\in\ZZ}|F_c(n)|^2
=\|Gc\|_{\ell^2}^2
=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\sigma(\theta)^2
|\widetilde c(\theta)|^2\dd\theta.
\]
Since $A_{\ZZ}\le \sigma\le B_{\ZZ}$, comparison with
\eqref{eq:lattice-symbol-form} yields
\[
A_{\ZZ}\|F_c\|_{\mathcal H_K}^2
\le
\sum_{n\in\ZZ}|F_c(n)|^2
\le
B_{\ZZ}\|F_c\|_{\mathcal H_K}^2.
\]
Passing to limits in $\mathcal H_K(\ZZ)$ proves
\eqref{eq:lattice-sampling-HK}.

The symbol bounds imply that $G$ extends to a boundedly invertible
operator on $\ell^2(\ZZ)$.  Thus for every $\alpha\in\ell^2(\ZZ)$ there is
a unique $c\in\ell^2(\ZZ)$ with $Gc=\alpha$.  By
\eqref{eq:lattice-riesz-kernel}, the series $\sum_n c_nK(\cdot,n)$
converges in $\mathcal H_K(\ZZ)$ to some $F_\alpha$, and then
$R_{\ZZ}F_\alpha=\alpha$.  Uniqueness follows from the lower bound in
\eqref{eq:lattice-sampling-HK}.
\fi

\subsection{Order-eight coefficient layer extracted from the finite-order coefficient calculus}%
\begin{lemma}[Uniform Laurent representatives through order six]%
\label{lem:n4-uniform-laurent-representatives}
For \(2\le n\le6\), the Laurent representative used in the eighth-order
coefficient calculation is the specialization of the single formula
\eqref{eq:mode-laurent-polynomial-definition}.  Equivalently, if
\(Q_n(z)=\sum_kq_{n,k}z^k\), then \(q_{n,0}=0\) and
\eqref{eq:mode-laurent-coefficients} gives every coefficient, including the
odd-mode signs on negative exponents.  Thus the relevant supports are
\[
S_j:=\operatorname{supp} Q_j=\{\pm1,\ldots,\pm j\},
\qquad 2\le j\le6.
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{lemma}[Laurent recurrence and coefficients through order eight]%
\label{lem:eighth-order-coefficient-arithmetic}
Use the Laurent representatives of
Lemma~\ref{lem:n4-uniform-laurent-representatives}.  If
\(Q_n(z)=\sum_kq_{n,k}z^k\), the constant term
\(J(\mathbf n)=[z^0]\prod_{j=1}^rQ_{n_j}(z)\) is produced directly from the
Laurent coefficients by the recurrence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:eighth-order-constant-term-recurrence}
C_0(s)=\mathbf 1_{\{s=0\}},\qquad
C_j(s)=\sum_{0<|k|\le n_j}q_{n_j,k}\,C_{j-1}(s-k)
\quad(1\le j\le r),\qquad J(\mathbf n)=C_r(0).
\end{equation}
Applying this recurrence to the rows entering \(A_4,A_6,A_8\) gives
\[
A_4=\frac{\sigma^4}{8},\qquad
A_6=\frac{\sigma^6+6\mu_3^2-8\sigma^2\mu_4}{64},
\]
and
\[
A_8=
\frac{3\sigma^8-12\sigma^4\mu_4+9\sigma^2\mu_3^2
+12\sigma^2\mu_6-30\mu_3\mu_5+20\mu_4^2}{256}.
\]
The full row-by-row finite certificate is recorded in
Appendix~\ref{app:order-eight-laurent-certificate}.
\end{lemma}

\begin{lemma}[Substitution into \(A_4\), \(A_6\), and \(A_8\)]%
\label{lem:n4-entropy-coefficient-substitution}
Let \(a_j=\mu_j u_j\) for \(2\le j\le6\), with \(\mu_2=\sigma^2\), and set
\[
d_t=t^{-2}a_2+t^{-3}a_3+t^{-4}a_4+t^{-5}a_5+t^{-6}a_6 .
\]
The coefficient polynomials before integration are
\[
\begin{array}{c|c}
\text{coefficient} & \text{polynomial before integration}\\
\hline
B_4 & \frac12a_2^2\\[0.25em]
B_6 & a_2a_4+\frac12a_3^2-\frac16a_2^3\\[0.25em]
B_8 &
a_2a_6+a_3a_5+\frac12a_4^2
-\frac12a_2^2a_4-\frac12a_2a_3^2+\frac1{12}a_2^4 .
\end{array}
\]
Using the recurrence values from
Lemma~\ref{lem:eighth-order-coefficient-arithmetic}, with the row certificate
displayed in Appendix~\ref{app:order-eight-laurent-certificate}, their
Cauchy integrals are
\[
A_4=\frac12\mu_2^2J(2,2)=\frac{\sigma^4}{8},
\]
\[
A_6=\mu_2\mu_4J(2,4)+\frac12\mu_3^2J(3,3)
-\frac16\mu_2^3J(2,2,2)
=\frac{\sigma^6+6\mu_3^2-8\sigma^2\mu_4}{64},
\]
\[
\begin{aligned}
A_8={}&\mu_2\mu_6J(2,6)+\mu_3\mu_5J(3,5)
+\frac12\mu_4^2J(4,4)\\
&-\frac12\mu_2^2\mu_4J(2,2,4)
-\frac12\mu_2\mu_3^2J(2,3,3)
+\frac1{12}\mu_2^4J(2,2,2,2)\\
={}&\frac{3\sigma^8-12\sigma^4\mu_4+9\sigma^2\mu_3^2
+12\sigma^2\mu_6-30\mu_3\mu_5+20\mu_4^2}{256}.
\end{aligned}
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{remark}[Normalization and two-point checks]
The representative \(Q_2\) in
Lemma~\ref{lem:n4-uniform-laurent-representatives} satisfies
\[
Q_2(1)=-1,
\]
agreeing with \(u_2(0)=-1\).  For the symmetric two-point law
\(\rho_\sigma=\tfrac12(\delta_{-\sigma}+\delta_\sigma)\), the moments are
\(\mu_2=\sigma^2\), \(\mu_3=\mu_5=0\), \(\mu_4=\sigma^4\), and
\(\mu_6=\sigma^6\).  Substitution into
Lemma~\ref{lem:n4-entropy-coefficient-substitution} gives
\[
A_6(\rho_\sigma)=\frac{1-8}{64}\sigma^6
=-\frac{7\sigma^6}{64},
\qquad
A_8(\rho_\sigma)=\frac{3-12+12+20}{256}\sigma^8
=\frac{23\sigma^8}{256}.
\]
These identities are sanity checks on the arithmetic for the already stated
coefficient-identification theorem below.
\end{remark}

\begin{lemma}[Eighth coefficient polynomial before coefficient identification]%
\label{lem:formal-seventh-eighth-coefficient}
The universal degree-eight coefficient polynomial involves only
\(\mathfrak m_2,\ldots,\mathfrak m_6\).  Its evaluation at the moments of
\(\nu\) is the polynomial fixed in
\eqref{eq:n4-notation-a8}:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:formal-eighth-evaluated-polynomial}
A_8(\nu)
:=
\frac{3\mu_2^4-12\mu_2^2\mu_4+9\mu_2\mu_3^2
+12\mu_2\mu_6-30\mu_3\mu_5+20\mu_4^2}{256}.
\end{equation}
At this stage \(A_8(\nu)\) is only a moment polynomial; its
entropy-coefficient status is supplied by
Theorem~\ref{thm:poisson-kl-even-orders}.
\end{lemma}

\begin{theorem}[Self-contained coefficient identification through order eight]%
\label{thm:poisson-kl-eighth}
Let $\nu$ be a probability measure on~$\RR$ with mean~$\bar{\gamma}$,
finite variance $\sigma^2\ge0$.  With
\[
X_c:=\gamma-\bar{\gamma},
\qquad
\mu_n:=\int_{\RR}X_c^n\dd\nu(\gamma)
\]
whenever the displayed moment exists, define
\[
A_6(\nu)
:=\frac{\sigma^6+6\mu_3^2-8\sigma^2\mu_4}{64}.
\]
When the moments through order six exist, define
\[
A_8(\nu)
:=
\frac{3\sigma^8-12\sigma^4\mu_4+9\sigma^2\mu_3^2
+12\sigma^2\mu_6-30\mu_3\mu_5+20\mu_4^2}{256}.
\]
Let
\[
h_t:=P_t*\nu,
\qquad
g_t(x):=P_t(x-\bar{\gamma}).
\]

\textup{(i) Sixth coefficient.}
If \(\nu\) has finite centred fourth absolute moment, then the \(t^{-6}\)
entropy coefficient exists and
\[
D_{\mathrm{KL}}(h_t\|g_t)
=\frac{\sigma^4}{8t^4}
+\frac{A_6(\nu)}{t^6}
+o(t^{-6}).
\]
Here \(A_6(\nu)\) is the \(t^{-6}\) entropy coefficient.

\textup{(ii) Eighth coefficient.}
If \(\nu\) has finite centred sixth absolute moment, then \(A_8(\nu)\) is the
\(t^{-8}\) entropy coefficient, and the expansion is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:poisson-kl-eighth}
\begin{split}
D_{\mathrm{KL}}(h_t\|g_t)
=\frac{\sigma^4}{8t^4}
+\frac{A_6(\nu)}{t^6}
\\
\quad
+\frac{A_8(\nu)}{t^8}
+o(t^{-8}).
\end{split}
\end{equation}
In this case \(A_6(\nu)\) is also the \(t^{-6}\) entropy coefficient.
The associated entropy-coefficient defects are
\[
\Delta_6(\nu)
:=-\frac{7}{64}\sigma^6-A_6(\nu),
\qquad
\mathcal E_8(\nu)
:=A_8(\nu)-\frac{23}{256}\sigma^8.
\]
These are the same unsuperscripted quantities defined in
\eqref{eq:n4-notation-a6}--\eqref{eq:n4-notation-e8}, now with their
entropy-coefficient status established under the hypotheses above.

These coefficients use the Laurent normalization of
\(Q_2,\ldots,Q_6\) fixed by
Lemma~\ref{lem:n4-uniform-laurent-representatives}, the constant-term
recurrence in Lemma~\ref{lem:eighth-order-coefficient-arithmetic}, the
certificate in Appendix~\ref{app:order-eight-laurent-certificate}, and the
coefficient substitution in
Lemma~\ref{lem:n4-entropy-coefficient-substitution}.  The normalization and
symmetric two-point checks are recorded in the preceding remark.  The finite
centred sixth absolute moment is exactly the \(N=4\) moment level in
Theorem~\ref{thm:poisson-kl-even-orders}; its scope is the boxed paragraph
following that theorem.

If \(\sigma^2=0\), then \(\nu=\delta_{\bar{\gamma}}\),
\(h_t=g_t\), all centred moments in the displayed formulas vanish, and both
coefficient expansions are the exact identity \(0=0\).  Positive variance is
therefore not needed for this raw coefficient identification.  It is reserved
below for normalized defect statements, where one fixes a nondegenerate
variance scale and uses \(\rho_\sigma\) or negative powers of \(\sigma\).
\end{theorem}

\begin{definition}[\(N=4,\eta=1\) retained-order constants]
\label{def:n4-finite-time-constant-dictionary}
Let \(\nu\) be a probability measure on \(\RR\) with mean \(\bar{\gamma}\),
centred moments \(\mu_n=\EE(\gamma-\bar{\gamma})^n\), and finite
\[
M_7(\nu):=\EE|\gamma-\bar{\gamma}|^7 .
\]
For the retained density order \(L=6\), set
\[
\mathcal M_{\nu,8}:=\sum_{n=2}^{6}(n+1)|\mu_n|,
\qquad
\mathcal C_{\nu,8}:=110M_7(\nu),
\]
\[
\mathcal S_{\nu,8}:=7\sum_{n=2}^{6}|\mu_n|+110M_7(\nu).
\]
Define
\[
\mathcal P_{\nu,8}:=
\sum_{r=2}^{4}\frac{1}{r(r-1)}
\sum_{\ell=1}^{r}\binom r\ell
\mathcal M_{\nu,8}^{\,r-\ell}\mathcal C_{\nu,8}^{\,\ell}
\]
and
\[
\mathcal Q_{\nu,8}:=
\sum_{r=2}^{4}\frac{1}{r(r-1)}
\sum_{m=9}^{6r}
\sum_{\substack{2\le n_1,\ldots,n_r\le6\\
n_1+\cdots+n_r=m}}
\prod_{j=1}^{r}\bigl((n_j+1)|\mu_{n_j}|\bigr),
\]
where an empty inner sum is zero.  The fixed-law order-nine remainder and
threshold constants used in
Theorem~\ref{thm:poisson-kl-eighth-seventh-remainder} are
\begin{equation}\label{eq:dictionary-fixed-law-n4-constants}
B_{\nu,8}:=\mathcal P_{\nu,8}+\mathcal Q_{\nu,8}
+2\mathcal S_{\nu,8}^{5},
\qquad
T_{\nu,8}:=\max\{1,(2\mathcal S_{\nu,8})^{1/2}\}.
\end{equation}

For the controlled shell \(\mu_2=\sigma^2\),
\(\EE|\gamma-\bar{\gamma}|^7\le M\), \(M\ge\sigma^7\), write
\(\widehat M=M/\sigma^7\).  For \(u\ge1\), set
\[
\mathcal M^{(8)}(u)
:=3+\sum_{n=3}^{6}(n+1)u^{n/7},
\qquad
\mathcal R^{(8)}(u):=110u,
\]
\[
\mathcal S^{(8)}(u)
:=7\left(1+\sum_{n=3}^{6}u^{n/7}\right)+110u .
\]
Define
\[
\mathcal P^{(8)}(u):=
\sum_{r=2}^{4}\frac{1}{r(r-1)}
\sum_{\ell=1}^{r}\binom r\ell
\bigl(\mathcal M^{(8)}(u)\bigr)^{r-\ell}
\bigl(\mathcal R^{(8)}(u)\bigr)^\ell
\]
and
\[
\mathcal Q^{(8)}(u):=
\sum_{r=2}^{4}\frac{1}{r(r-1)}
\sum_{m=9}^{6r}
\sum_{\substack{2\le n_1,\ldots,n_r\le6\\
n_1+\cdots+n_r=m}}
\prod_{j=1}^{r}(n_j+1)\,\Lambda_{n_j}(u),
\]
with
\[
\Lambda_2(u):=1,\qquad
\Lambda_n(u):=u^{n/7}\quad(3\le n\le6).
\]
The normalized controlled-shell functions are
\begin{equation}\label{eq:dictionary-normalized-shell-functions}
\begin{aligned}
C(u)
&:=\frac{1}{256}\left(
3+12u^{4/7}+21u^{6/7}+50u^{8/7}
\right),\\
B(u)
&:=\mathcal P^{(8)}(u)+\mathcal Q^{(8)}(u)
+2\bigl(\mathcal S^{(8)}(u)\bigr)^5,\\
T(u)
&:=\max\{1,\bigl(2\mathcal S^{(8)}(u)\bigr)^{1/2}\}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Their dimensional scale restores are
\begin{equation}\label{eq:dictionary-controlled-shell-c8}
C_{\sigma,M}^{(8)}:=\sigma^8C(\widehat M),\qquad
B_{\sigma,M}^{(8)}:=\sigma^9B(\widehat M),\qquad
T_{\sigma,M}^{(8)}:=\sigma T(\widehat M).
\end{equation}
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Quantitative \(N=4\) remainder under the seventh moment]%
\label{thm:poisson-kl-eighth-seventh-remainder}
Let $\nu$ be a probability measure on~$\RR$ with mean~$\bar{\gamma}$,
finite variance $\sigma^2\ge0$, and finite centred seventh absolute moment.  Use the
notation of Theorem~\ref{thm:poisson-kl-eighth}; in particular
\(A_6(\nu)\) and \(A_8(\nu)\) are the identified entropy coefficients there.
Let \(B_{\nu,8}\) and \(T_{\nu,8}\) be the explicit constants in
\eqref{eq:dictionary-fixed-law-n4-constants}.  Then for every
\(t\ge T_{\nu,8}\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:poisson-kl-eighth-remainder}
\begin{split}
\Bigg|D_{\mathrm{KL}}(h_t\|g_t)
&-\frac{\sigma^4}{8t^4}
-\frac{\sigma^6+6\mu_3^2-8\sigma^2\mu_4}{64\,t^6}
\\
&-\frac{3\sigma^8-12\sigma^4\mu_4+9\sigma^2\mu_3^2
+12\sigma^2\mu_6-30\mu_3\mu_5+20\mu_4^2}{256\,t^8}
\Bigg|
\le B_{\nu,8}t^{-9}.
\end{split}
\end{equation}
Equivalently, the coefficient-identifying expansion
\eqref{eq:poisson-kl-eighth} improves in this positive-margin regime to
\[
D_{\mathrm{KL}}(h_t\|g_t)
=\frac{\sigma^4}{8t^4}
+\frac{A_6(\nu)}{t^6}
+\frac{A_8(\nu)}{t^8}
+O_\nu(t^{-9}).
\]
For \(\sigma^2=0\), this is again the exact degenerate identity \(0=0\);
the positive-variance restrictions in this subsection occur only in
normalized shells and defect inequalities.
\end{theorem}

\begin{remark}
The finite centred sixth absolute moment is the \(N=4\) hypothesis
\(2N-2=6\).  The seventh moment enters
Theorem~\ref{thm:poisson-kl-eighth-seventh-remainder} only for the
quantitative \(O(t^{-9})\) remainder.

The entropy proof uses the centred moment jet and, for the quantitative
remainder, the seventh absolute moment bound directly.  No finite-grid,
lattice-reconstruction, or finite-time approximation input is used in the
entropy theorem chain.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[Moment-jet factorization of the eighth-order coefficients]%
\label{cor:eighth-order-jet-factorization}
Let $\nu$ and $\widetilde\nu$ be probability measures on~$\RR$ with means
$\bar{\gamma}$ and $\widetilde{\bar{\gamma}}$, positive variances, and finite
centred sixth absolute moments.  Write
\[
\mu_n:=\int(\gamma-\bar{\gamma})^n\dd\nu(\gamma),
\qquad
\widetilde\mu_n:=\int(\gamma-\widetilde{\bar{\gamma}})^n
\dd\widetilde\nu(\gamma).
\]
If
\[
\mu_n=\widetilde\mu_n,
\qquad 2\le n\le6,
\]
then the two matched-mean Poisson entropy expansions have the same retained
eighth-order coefficient jet:
\[
A_6(\nu)=A_6(\widetilde\nu),
\qquad
A_8(\nu)=A_8(\widetilde\nu).
\]
Equivalently, the entropy coefficients \(A_6\) and
\(A_8\) in Theorem~\ref{thm:poisson-kl-eighth}, and hence the
entropy-coefficient comparison coordinates introduced below after coefficient identification,
factor through the finite centred moment jet $(\mu_2,\ldots,\mu_6)$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{corollary}[Moment-jet equality with seventh-moment remainder]%
\label{cor:eighth-order-jet-factorization-seventh-remainder}
Let $\nu$ and $\widetilde\nu$ satisfy the hypotheses of
Corollary~\ref{cor:eighth-order-jet-factorization}.  Assume in addition that
both laws have finite centred seventh absolute moments.  If
\[
\mu_n=\widetilde\mu_n,
\qquad 2\le n\le6,
\]
then
\[
D_{\mathrm{KL}}(P_t*\nu\|P_t(\cdot-\bar{\gamma}))
-D_{\mathrm{KL}}(P_t*\widetilde\nu\|P_t(\cdot-\widetilde{\bar{\gamma}}))
=O_{\nu,\widetilde\nu}(t^{-9}).
\]
\end{corollary}

\begin{corollary}[A two-level defect ladder]%
\label{thm:poisson-defect-ladder}
\label{cor:coefficient-defect-coupling}
Let $\nu$ be a probability measure on~$\RR$ with mean~$\bar{\gamma}$,
variance $\sigma^2>0$, and finite centred sixth absolute moment.  Use the
coefficient notation above, after the identifications of
Theorem~\ref{thm:poisson-kl-even-orders} with \(N=4\).
Let $X:=(\gamma-\bar{\gamma})/\sigma$ on the probability space
$(\RR,\nu)$, set
\[
\beta_n:=\EE[X^n],\qquad n\in\NN,\ n\ge1,\qquad
\beta_3:=\EE[X^3],
\qquad
Q_2:=X^2-1-\beta_3X,
\]
and define
\[
Q_3:=XQ_2-\|Q_2\|_{L^2(\nu)}^2X-\frac{\beta_3}{4}Q_2.
\]
Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:defect-c6}
\frac{64}{\sigma^6}A_6(\nu)
=-7-2\beta_3^2-8\|Q_2\|_{L^2(\nu)}^2,
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:defect-c8}
\frac{256}{\sigma^8}A_8(\nu)
=23
+12\|Q_3\|_{L^2(\nu)}^2
+32\|Q_2\|_{L^2(\nu)}^4
+\frac{61}{4}\beta_3^2\|Q_2\|_{L^2(\nu)}^2
+52\|Q_2\|_{L^2(\nu)}^2
+2\beta_3^4
+13\beta_3^2.
\end{equation}
In particular
\begin{equation}\label{eq:defect-c8-lower}
A_8(\nu)\ge \frac{23}{256}\sigma^8,
\end{equation}
and equality holds if and only if
\[
\nu=\frac12\delta_{\bar{\gamma}+\sigma}
+\frac12\delta_{\bar{\gamma}-\sigma}.
\]
The identity \eqref{eq:defect-c6} recovers the classical Pearson
moment relation, while \eqref{eq:defect-c8} gives the next higher-order
component in the same coefficient comparison coordinates.
\end{corollary}

\begin{corollary}[Coefficient excess amplification]\label{thm:poisson-defect-amplification}
Let $\nu$ be a probability measure on~$\RR$ with mean~$\bar{\gamma}$,
variance $\sigma^2>0$, and finite centred sixth absolute moment.  Define
\[
\Delta_6(\nu)
:=-\frac{7}{64}\sigma^6-A_6(\nu),
\qquad
\mathcal E_8(\nu)
:=A_8(\nu)-\frac{23}{256}\sigma^8,
\]
with \(A_6\) and \(A_8\) the entropy coefficients identified in
Theorem~\ref{thm:poisson-kl-even-orders}.  Set
\[
X:=\frac{\gamma-\bar{\gamma}}{\sigma},
\qquad
\beta_3:=\EE[X^3],
\qquad
Q_2:=X^2-1-\beta_3X,
\]
\[
\kappa:=\|Q_2\|_{L^2(\nu)}^2,
\qquad
Q_3:=XQ_2-\kappa X-\frac{\beta_3}{4}Q_2.
\]
Then
\[
\Delta_6(\nu)
=\frac{\sigma^6}{32}\beta_3^2+\frac{\sigma^6}{8}\kappa,
\]
and
\begin{align}
\mathcal E_8(\nu)
&=
\frac{13\sigma^2}{8}\Delta_6(\nu)
\notag\\
&\quad
+\frac{\sigma^8}{256}\left(
12\|Q_3\|_{L^2(\nu)}^2
+32\kappa^2
+\frac{61}{4}\beta_3^2\kappa
+2\beta_3^4
\right). \label{eq:defect-amplification}
\end{align}
In particular,
\[
\mathcal E_8(\nu)\ge
\frac{13\sigma^2}{8}\Delta_6(\nu),
\]
and equality in this proved coefficient layer through order eight holds if and only if
\[
\nu=\frac12\delta_{\bar{\gamma}+\sigma}
+\frac12\delta_{\bar{\gamma}-\sigma}.
\]
\end{corollary}

\begin{corollary}[Symmetric two-point coefficient rigidity]%
\label{cor:poisson-two-point-defect-rigidity}
Let $\nu$ be a probability measure on~$\RR$ with mean~$\bar{\gamma}$,
variance $\sigma^2>0$, and finite centred sixth absolute moment.  Use
\(A_6(\nu)\) and \(A_8(\nu)\) as the entropy coefficients identified in
Theorem~\ref{thm:poisson-kl-even-orders}.
Define
\[
\Delta_6(\nu)
:=-\frac{7}{64}\sigma^6-A_6(\nu),
\qquad
\mathcal E_8(\nu)
:=A_8(\nu)-\frac{23}{256}\sigma^8.
\]
Let $\nu_c$ be the centred law of $\gamma-\bar{\gamma}$ and set
\[
\rho_\sigma:=\frac12(\delta_{-\sigma}+\delta_\sigma).
\]
Then, among all laws with the same mean and variance in this moment class,
\[
A_6(\nu)\le -\frac{7}{64}\sigma^6,
\qquad
A_8(\nu)\ge \frac{23}{256}\sigma^8.
\]
Moreover the following conditions are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)},leftmargin=*]
\item $\nu_c=\rho_\sigma$, equivalently
$\nu=\frac12\delta_{\bar{\gamma}-\sigma}
+\frac12\delta_{\bar{\gamma}+\sigma}$;
\item \(A_6(\nu)=-\frac{7}{64}\sigma^6\);
\item \(A_8(\nu)=\frac{23}{256}\sigma^8\);
\item \(\Delta_6(\nu)=
\mathcal E_8(\nu)=0\);
\item the amplification bound is sharp,
\[
\mathcal E_8(\nu)
=\frac{13\sigma^2}{8}\Delta_6(\nu).
\]
\end{enumerate}
\end{corollary}

\begin{remark}
This rigidity statement is only for the coefficient layer through order eight proved in
this paper.  No all-order defect hierarchy is asserted here.
\end{remark}

\section{Mode Closure, Gaussian Compression, and Dimensional Thresholds}
\subsection{The first Cayley channel}
\begin{theorem}[First-channel closure and the even-moment hierarchy]
\label{thm:first-cayley-mode-closure}
Let \(\nu\) be a probability law on \(\RR\) with finite mean, and use the
Cayley coordinate
\[
 z=\frac{1+\ii y}{1-\ii y}\in\TT\setminus\{-1\}.
\]
Then, for every \(t>0\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:first-channel-exact-fourier-series}
 \frac{(P_t*\nu)(\bar\gamma+ty)}{P_t(ty)}
 =1+\sum_{k\ge1}
 \bigl(\overline{c_k(t)}z^k+c_k(t)z^{-k}\bigr).
\end{equation}
The series converges pointwise for every \(y\in\RR\), locally uniformly in
\(y\), and in \(L^2(\omega)\) whenever \(\EE X^2<\infty\).  Its coefficients
satisfy the exact differential closure
\begin{equation}\label{eq:first-channel-differential-closure}
 c_{k+1}(t)=-c_k(t)-\frac{t}{k}c_k'(t),
 \qquad k\ge1.
\end{equation}
Consequently, the scale trajectory \(c_1\), with its successive derivatives,
determines the matched quotient of the centred law at every scale.  Under
finite variance one also has
\begin{equation}\label{eq:first-channel-chi-square-parseval}
 \chi^2\!\left(P_t*\nu\,\middle\|\,
 P_t(\mathord\cdot-\bar\gamma)\right)
 =2\sum_{k\ge1}|c_k(t)|^2.
\end{equation}
If \(\varphi_X(s)=\EE e^{\ii sX}\), then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:first-channel-laplace-resolvent}
 \frac{1+c_1(t)}{2t}
 =\EE\frac1{2t-\ii X}
 =\int_0^\infty e^{-2ts}\varphi_X(s)\,\dd s.
\end{equation}
Thus the full scale trajectory identifies the centred law by the classical
uniqueness of the Laplace and characteristic transforms.  It identifies the
untranslated law \(\nu\) only when \(\bar\gamma\) is supplied separately.

Moreover, if \(n\ge1\) and \(\mu_{2j}=\EE X^{2j}<\infty\) for
\(1\le j<n\), then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:first-channel-even-moment-remainder}
\begin{aligned}
 &(-1)^{n-1}4^nt^{2n}
 \left[-\Re c_1(t)-\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}
 (-1)^{j-1}\frac{\mu_{2j}}{4^jt^{2j}}\right]\\
 &\hspace{32mm}
 =\EE\!\left[\frac{X^{2n}}{1+X^2/(4t^2)}\right]
 \uparrow\EE|X|^{2n}
 \quad(t\to\infty),
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where the limit is permitted to be \(+\infty\).
\end{theorem}

\subsection{The infinite critical-moment boundary is not spectrally identifiable}
\begin{lemma}[Finite-covariance Fatou lower bound]
\label{lem:finite-covariance-fatou-lower-bound}
Let \(d\ge1\), and let \(\nu\) be a centred probability law on \(\RR^d\)
with finite covariance matrix \(\Sigma\).  Then, with the relative entropy
understood in the extended sense,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:finite-covariance-fatou-lower-bound}
 \liminf_{t\to\infty}t^4H_d(t)\ge \mathcal Q_d(\Sigma).
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{theorem}[Critical vague tails do not identify the leading entropy]
\label{thm:critical-vague-tail-nonidentifiability}
Fix \(d\ge4\), and set
\[
 p=\frac{4(d+1)}{d+5},\qquad
 q=\frac{d+5}{d+1}=1+\frac4{d+1};
\]
thus \(pq=4\).  Let \(\sigma\) be normalized surface measure on
\(S^{d-1}\), and let \(L(r)=\log r\).  For every positive-definite matrix
\(\Sigma\), there are two centred probability laws
\(\nu_{\rm ac}\) and \(\nu_{\rm cl}\) with covariance \(\Sigma\) such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:critical-vague-tail-common-limit}
 \frac{r^p}{\log r}\,\nu_\bullet(r\,\mathord\cdot)
 \xrightarrow[r\to\infty]{v}\mu_\sigma
 \quad\text{on }\RR^d\setminus\{0\},
 \qquad \bullet\in\{\mathrm{ac},\mathrm{cl}\},
\end{equation}
but
\begin{equation}\label{eq:critical-vague-tail-contrasting-entropy}
 t^4H_{d,{\rm ac}}(t)\longrightarrow\mathcal Q_d(\Sigma),
 \qquad
 \limsup_{t\to\infty}t^4H_{d,{\rm cl}}(t)=+\infty.
\end{equation}
Consequently, the spectral measure and the slowly varying factor do not
determine whether the covariance lower bound is an asymptotic equality,
even when the spectral measure is smooth, everywhere nondegenerate, and
antipodally symmetric.
\end{theorem}

\subsection{Moment-matched comparisons and Gaussian compression}
\begin{theorem}[First unmatched moment for Poisson relative entropy]
\label{thm:moment-matched-poisson-kl}
Let \(r\ge2\), and let \(\nu,\eta\) be probability laws on \(\RR\) with
finite \(r\)th absolute moments.  Suppose
\[
 m_j(\nu)=m_j(\eta)\quad(0\le j<r),
 \qquad \Delta_r:=m_r(\nu)-m_r(\eta)\ne0.
\]
Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:moment-matched-poisson-kl}
 D_{\rm KL}(P_t*\nu\|P_t*\eta)
 =C_r\Delta_r^2t^{-2r}+o(t^{-2r}),
 \qquad
 C_r=4^{-r}\binom{2r-2}{r-1}.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}[Gaussian-comparison entropy threshold]
\label{thm:gauss-poisson-kl-threshold}
Let \(n\ge1\), and let \(\nu\) be a probability law on \(\RR\) satisfying
\[
 \EE|X|^{2n-1}<\infty,
 \qquad |\operatorname{supp}\nu|\ge n.
\]
Let \(\pi_n\) be the monic degree-\(n\) polynomial orthogonal to
\(1,x,\ldots,x^{n-1}\) with respect to \(\nu\), and let \(G_n\nu\) be its
\(n\)-node Gaussian quadrature law.  Thus
\begin{equation}\label{eq:gauss-moment-matching}
 m_j(G_n\nu)=m_j(\nu),\qquad 0\le j\le2n-1.
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:gauss-poisson-finite-limit}
 \EE|X|^{2n}<\infty
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \lim_{t\to\infty}t^{4n}
 D_{\rm KL}(P_t*\nu\|P_t*G_n\nu)
 =4^{-2n}\binom{4n-2}{2n-1}
 \|\pi_n\|_{L^2(\nu)}^4,
\end{equation}
whereas
\begin{equation}\label{eq:gauss-poisson-infinite-limit}
 \EE|X|^{2n}=\infty
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 t^{4n}D_{\rm KL}(P_t*\nu\|P_t*G_n\nu)\longrightarrow+\infty.
\end{equation}
Consequently,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:gauss-poisson-moment-equivalence}
 \EE|X|^{2n}<\infty
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \limsup_{t\to\infty}t^{4n}
 D_{\rm KL}(P_t*\nu\|P_t*G_n\nu)<\infty.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{remark}[Classical optimal degree]
The assertion that an \(n\)-node positive quadrature rule has maximal exact
degree \(2n-1\), and that Gaussian quadrature attains this degree, is a
classical quadrature theorem, not a new result of this article.  The node
polynomial gives the elementary obstruction at degree \(2n\); see
\cite{GolubWelsch1969GaussQuadrature}.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[Two-node fourth-moment coefficient]
\label{cor:two-node-gauss-fourth-moment}
Assume
\[
 \EE X=0,\qquad \EE X^2=1,\qquad \EE|X|^3<\infty,
\]
and put
\[
 \beta_3=\EE X^3,\qquad
 \pi_2(x)=x^2-\beta_3x-1.
\]
Let \(G_2\nu\) be the two-node Gaussian quadrature law.  If
\(\EE X^4<\infty\), then, with
\[
 \kappa=\|\pi_2\|_{L^2(\nu)}^2
 =\EE X^4-1-\beta_3^2,
\]
one has
\begin{equation}\label{eq:two-node-fourth-moment-coefficient}
 \lim_{t\to\infty}t^8D_{\rm KL}(P_t*\nu\|P_t*G_2\nu)
 =\frac5{64}\kappa^2.
\end{equation}
If \(\EE X^4=\infty\), the left side of
\eqref{eq:two-node-fourth-moment-coefficient} tends to \(+\infty\).  For a
symmetric law,
\(G_2\nu=(\delta_{-1}+\delta_1)/2\) and
\(\kappa=\EE X^4-1\).
\end{corollary}

\begin{theorem}[Square boundary at a missing Gaussian moment]
\label{thm:gauss-regular-variation-square-law}
Let \(n\ge2\), put \(p=2n\), and let \(\nu\) satisfy the hypotheses of
Theorem~\ref{thm:gauss-poisson-kl-threshold}.  Assume, for an eventually
positive slowly varying function \(L\) and constants \(c_+,c_-\ge0\) with
\(c_++c_->0\),
\[
 \mathbb P(X>x)\sim c_+x^{-p}L(x),\qquad
 \mathbb P(X<-x)\sim c_-x^{-p}L(x),
\]
and assume
\[
 \ell_L(t)=\int_{x_0}^t\frac{L(s)}s\dd s\longrightarrow\infty.
\]
Then, with
\(M_p(t)=\EE[|X|^p\mathbf1_{\{|X|\le t\}}]\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:gauss-regular-variation-square-law}
 D_{\rm KL}(P_t*\nu\|P_t*G_n\nu)
 \sim C_p t^{-2p}M_p(t)^2,
 \qquad C_p=4^{-p}\binom{2p-2}{p-1}.
\end{equation}
In particular,
\[
 M_p(t)\sim p(c_++c_-)\ell_L(t),
\]
so the boundary normalization is the square of the integrated slowly
varying factor and the leading constant is positive.
\end{theorem}

\subsection{The multidimensional \texorpdfstring{\(L^2\)}{L2} and entropy
\begin{lemma}[Large translations in the Cauchy quotient space]
\label{lem:multidim-large-translation-l2-growth}
For every \(d\ge1\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:translated-quotient-l2-asymptotic}
 \|F_\cdot(z)\|_{L^2(\Omega_d)}^2
 \sim2^{-d}|z|^{d+1}\qquad(|z|\to\infty).
\end{equation}
If
\[
 q_d:=\max\left\{2,\frac{d+1}{2}\right\},
\]
then a constant \(C_d\) exists such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:second-order-l2-global-remainder}
 \|F_\cdot(z)-T_2(\cdot,z)\|_{L^2(\Omega_d)}
 \le C_d
 \begin{cases}|z|^3,&|z|\le1,\\ |z|^{q_d},&|z|>1.
 \end{cases}
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{theorem}[Sharp absolute-moment condition for the \(L^2\) quotient]
\label{thm:multidim-l2-moment-threshold}
Let \(\nu\) be a centred probability law on \(\RR^d\), with covariance
matrix \(\Sigma\).  If
\begin{equation}\label{eq:multidim-qd-moment}
 \EE|X|^{q_d}<\infty,
 \qquad q_d=\max\left\{2,\frac{d+1}{2}\right\},
\end{equation}
then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:multidim-l2-second-order-convergence}
 t^2\delta_t^{(d)}\longrightarrow b_\Sigma
 \quad\text{in }L^2(\Omega_d).
\end{equation}
For \(d\ge4\), this exponent is sharp among hypotheses stated solely as a
finite absolute moment: for every \(2\le p<q_d\), there is a centred law
with positive-definite covariance and finite \(p\)th absolute moment for
which \eqref{eq:multidim-l2-second-order-convergence} fails.
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}[Low-dimensional finite-covariance entropy threshold]
\label{thm:low-dimensional-finite-covariance-threshold}
Let \(1\le d\le3\), and let \(\nu\) be a probability law on \(\RR^d\) with
finite mean.  With
\[
 H_d(t)=D_{\rm KL}\!\left(P_t^{(d)}*\nu\,\middle\|\,
 P_t^{(d)}(\mathord\cdot-\bar\gamma)\right),
\]
one has
\begin{equation}\label{eq:low-dimensional-covariance-iff}
 \EE|X|^2<\infty
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \limsup_{t\to\infty}t^4H_d(t)<\infty.
\end{equation}
If the covariance is finite, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:low-dimensional-covariance-limit}
 H_d(t)=\mathcal Q_d(\Sigma)t^{-4}+o(t^{-4}).
\end{equation}
If it is infinite, then \(t^4H_d(t)\to+\infty\) in the extended sense.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proposition}[Finite covariance does not control high-dimensional KL]
\label{prop:high-dimensional-finite-covariance-obstruction}
For every \(d\ge4\), there exists a centred discrete probability law on
\(\RR^d\) with positive-definite finite covariance such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:high-dimensional-finite-covariance-obstruction}
 \limsup_{t\to\infty}t^4H_d(t)=+\infty.
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{lemma}[Critical translation remainder]
\label{lem:critical-lq-translation-remainder}
Let \(d\ge4\), let \(p_d,q_d\) be as in
\eqref{eq:critical-kl-exponents}, and put \(m=\lfloor p_d\rfloor\).
If \(T_m(y,z)\) is the Taylor polynomial of \(F_y(z)\) through degree
\(m\), then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:critical-lq-exact-translation-norm}
 \|F_\cdot(z)\|_{L^{q_d}(\Omega_d)}^{q_d}
 =1+\frac{2(d+5)}{(d+1)(d+3)}|z|^2
 +\frac{3}{(d+1)(d+3)}|z|^4.
\end{equation}
Consequently, for a constant depending only on \(d\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:critical-lq-global-remainder}
 \|F_\cdot(z)-T_m(\cdot,z)\|_{L^{q_d}(\Omega_d)}
 \le C_d
 \begin{cases}
 |z|^{m+1},& |z|\le1,\\
 |z|^{p_d},& |z|>1,
 \end{cases}
 \le C_d|z|^{p_d}.
\end{equation}
Here \(m=2\) for \(4\le d\le10\), while \(m=3\) for \(d\ge11\),
including the integer endpoint \(p_{11}=3\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{lemma}[Stable critical translation remainder]
\label{lem:stable-critical-translation-remainder}
Let \(d\ge1\), \(0<\alpha<2\), \(p=p_{\alpha,d}\),
\(q=q_{\alpha,d}\), and \(m=\lfloor p\rfloor\).  If
\(T_m^{(\alpha,d)}(y,z)\) is the Taylor polynomial of
\(F_y^{(\alpha,d)}(z)\) at \(z=0\) through degree \(m\), then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:stable-translation-lq-growth}
 c_{\alpha,d,q}(1+|z|)^{\beta(q-1)}
 \le \|F_\cdot^{(\alpha,d)}(z)\|_{L^q(\Omega_{\alpha,d})}^q
 \le C_{\alpha,d,q}(1+|z|)^{\beta(q-1)}.
\end{equation}
Moreover,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:stable-critical-global-remainder}
 \|F_\cdot^{(\alpha,d)}(z)-T_m^{(\alpha,d)}(\cdot,z)\|_{L^q(\Omega_{\alpha,d})}
 \le C_{\alpha,d}
 \begin{cases}
  |z|^{m+1},&|z|\le1,\\
  |z|^p,&|z|>1,
 \end{cases}
 \le C_{\alpha,d}|z|^p.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{lemma}[Critical Bregman perturbation bound]
\label{lem:critical-lq-kl-perturbation}
Let \(1<q\le2\) and
\(\Phi(s)=(1+s)\log(1+s)-s\) on \([-1,\infty)\), with
\(0\log0=0\).  There is a constant \(C_q\) such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:critical-bregman-pointwise-bound}
 0\le \Phi(u+v)-\Phi(u)-\log(1+u)v\le C_q|v|^q
\end{equation}
whenever \(|u|\le1/4\) and \(u+v\ge-1\).
\end{lemma}

\begin{theorem}[Optimal uniform sufficient moment exponent for stable KL asymptotics]
\label{thm:high-dimensional-kl-moment-threshold}
Let \(d\ge1\), \(0<\alpha<2\), and let \(\nu\) be a probability law on
\(\RR^d\) with mean \(\bar\gamma\).  Put
\(X=\gamma-\bar\gamma\), and assume
\begin{equation}\label{eq:critical-kl-moment-hypothesis}
 \EE|X|^{p_{\alpha,d}}<\infty,
 \qquad
 p_{\alpha,d}=\max\left\{2,
 \frac{4(d+\alpha)}{d+\alpha+4}\right\},
\end{equation}
then the covariance \(\Sigma=\EE[XX^{\mathsf T}]\) is finite and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:critical-kl-leading-asymptotic}
 H_{\alpha,d}(s):=
 D_{\rm KL}\!\left(p_s^{(\alpha,d)}*\nu\,\middle\|\,
 p_s^{(\alpha,d)}(\mathord\cdot-\bar\gamma)\right)
 =\mathcal Q_{\alpha,d}(\Sigma)s^{-4}+o(s^{-4}),
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}\label{eq:stable-leading-covariance-functional}
 b_{\alpha,d,\Sigma}(y)
 :=\frac1{2p_1^{(\alpha,d)}(y)}
   \sum_{i,j=1}^d\Sigma_{ij}\partial_{ij}p_1^{(\alpha,d)}(y),
 \qquad
 \mathcal Q_{\alpha,d}(\Sigma)
 :=\frac12\int b_{\alpha,d,\Sigma}^2\dd\Omega_{\alpha,d}.
\end{equation}
The exponent is optimal only in the following uniform sense.  For every
\(0<r<2\), there is a mean-zero law with finite \(r\)th absolute moment
and \(\limsup_{s\to\infty}s^4H_{\alpha,d}(s)=+\infty\).  If
\(d+\alpha>4\), then for every \(2\le r<p_{\alpha,d}\) the counterexample
may additionally be chosen with positive-definite finite covariance.
Thus no smaller moment exponent implies
\eqref{eq:critical-kl-leading-asymptotic} uniformly over its entire moment
class, and the endpoint belongs to the sufficient side.

This is not a law-by-law moment criterion.  When \(d+\alpha>4\), finite
\(p_{\alpha,d}\)-moment is not asserted to be necessary for an individual
law; the exact finite-covariance criterion is the nonlinear raw-tail energy
in Theorem~\ref{thm:stable-law-by-law-decomposition} below.  When
\(d+\alpha\le4\), the exponent is two and boundedness of
\(s^4H_{\alpha,d}(s)\) does force finite covariance, as the proof below
shows.  For \(\alpha=1\) with the standard normalization
\(p_1^{(1,d)}=P_1^{(d)}\), one has
\(\mathcal Q_{1,d}=\mathcal Q_d\), so the theorem contains the radial
Poisson result above.
\end{theorem}

\subsection{The law-by-law finite-covariance decomposition}
\begin{theorem}[Stable law-by-law tail decomposition]
\label{thm:stable-law-by-law-decomposition}
Let \(d\ge1\), \(0<\alpha<2\), and let \(\nu\) be a centred probability law
on \(\RR^d\) with finite covariance matrix \(\Sigma\).  Put
\[
 H_{\alpha,d}(s):=
 D_{\rm KL}\!\left(p_s^{(\alpha,d)}*\nu\,\middle\|\,
 p_s^{(\alpha,d)}\right)
\]
and define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:stable-raw-tail-potential}
 V_s^\nu(y):=\int_{\{|x|>s\}}
 \frac{p_1^{(\alpha,d)}(y-x/s)}
      {p_1^{(\alpha,d)}(y)}\,\nu(\dd x).
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:stable-law-by-law-decomposition}
 H_{\alpha,d}(s)=\mathcal Q_{\alpha,d}(\Sigma)s^{-4}
 +\int_{\RR^d}\Phi(V_s^\nu(y))\,\Omega_{\alpha,d}(\dd y)
 +o(s^{-4}).
\end{equation}
Consequently,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:stable-law-by-law-criterion}
 s^4H_{\alpha,d}(s)\longrightarrow\mathcal Q_{\alpha,d}(\Sigma)
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 s^4\int_{\RR^d}\Phi(V_s^\nu)\dd\Omega_{\alpha,d}\longrightarrow0.
\end{equation}
No regular-variation, absolute-continuity, or moment hypothesis above order two
is imposed.
\end{theorem}

\begin{remark}[What the decomposition resolves]
When \(d+\alpha\le4\), one has \(p_{\alpha,d}=2\), so every
finite-covariance law makes the tail energy \(o(s^{-4})\) by
Theorem~\ref{thm:high-dimensional-kl-moment-threshold}.  When
\(d+\alpha>4\), finite covariance alone need not do so.  In that regime
\eqref{eq:stable-law-by-law-criterion} is the exact missing law-by-law
condition: it neither replaces the nonlinear tail energy by a moment nor
assumes regular variation.
\end{remark}

\section{The Covariance-Matched Poisson Defect}
\begin{theorem}[Covariance-proxy defect decomposition]
\label{thm:covariance-proxy-defect-decomposition}
\label{thm:finite-covariance-proxy-defect-decomposition}
Let \(d\ge1\), and let \(\nu\) be a probability law on \(\RR^d\) with mean
\(\bar\gamma\) and finite covariance matrix \(\Sigma\).  Put
\[
 h_t=P_t^{(d)}*\nu,
 \qquad g_t=P_t^{(d)}(\mathord\cdot-\bar\gamma),
 \qquad H_d(t)=D_{\rm KL}(h_t\|g_t),
\]
and let \(b_\Sigma\) be the bounded covariance mode in
\eqref{eq:multidim-leading-theorem-density-mode}.  For all sufficiently
large \(t\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:covariance-poisson-proxy}
 k_{\Sigma,t}(x)
 :=g_t(x)\left[1+t^{-2}b_\Sigma
 \left(\frac{x-\bar\gamma}{t}\right)\right]
\end{equation}
is a probability density.  Define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:covariance-proxy-defect-functional}
 \mathcal C_{\nu,\Sigma}(t)
 :=t^4D_{\rm KL}(h_t\|k_{\Sigma,t}).
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:covariance-proxy-defect-decomposition}
 t^4H_d(t)
 =\mathcal Q_d(\Sigma)+\mathcal C_{\nu,\Sigma}(t)+o(1)
 \qquad(t\to\infty).
\end{equation}
Consequently,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:covariance-proxy-defect-equivalence}
 t^4H_d(t)\longrightarrow\mathcal Q_d(\Sigma)
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \mathcal C_{\nu,\Sigma}(t)\longrightarrow0.
\end{equation}
No density of \(\nu\), regular-variation hypothesis, angular balance, or
higher absolute moment is required.
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}[Raw-tail Poisson-energy decomposition]
\label{thm:raw-tail-poisson-energy-decomposition}
Let \(d\ge1\), and let \(\nu\) be a probability law on \(\RR^d\) with
mean \(\bar\gamma\) and finite covariance matrix \(\Sigma\).  Write
\(\nu_c\) for its centred law, put \(a=(d+1)/2\), and define
\[
 \Omega_d(\dd y)=P_1^{(d)}(y)\dd y,
 \qquad
 F_y(z)=\left(\frac{1+|y|^2}{1+|y-z|^2}\right)^a,
 \qquad
 \Phi(v)=(1+v)\log(1+v)-v.
\]
For \(t>0\), let
\begin{equation}\label{eq:raw-tail-poisson-potential}
 V_t^\nu(y):=\int_{\{|x|>t\}}F_y(x/t)\,\nu_c(\dd x).
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:raw-tail-entropy-decomposition}
 H_d(t)=\mathcal Q_d(\Sigma)t^{-4}
 +\int_{\RR^d}\Phi(V_t^\nu)\dd\Omega_d+o(t^{-4}).
\end{equation}
Consequently, the covariance-proxy defect in
\eqref{eq:covariance-proxy-defect-functional} satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:raw-tail-proxy-decomposition}
 \mathcal C_{\nu,\Sigma}(t)
 =t^4\int_{\RR^d}\Phi(V_t^\nu)\dd\Omega_d+o(1),
\end{equation}
and hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:raw-tail-proxy-equivalence}
 \mathcal C_{\nu,\Sigma}(t)\longrightarrow0
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 t^4\int_{\RR^d}\Phi(V_t^\nu)\dd\Omega_d\longrightarrow0.
\end{equation}
No regular-variation or absolute-continuity hypothesis is required.
\end{theorem}

\begin{corollary}[Moving-annulus raw-tail criterion]
\label{cor:moving-annulus-tail-criterion}
Under the hypotheses of
Theorem~\ref{thm:raw-tail-poisson-energy-decomposition},
\[
 t^4H_d(t)\longrightarrow\mathcal Q_d(\Sigma)
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \mathcal C_{\nu,\Sigma}(t)\longrightarrow0
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 G_\nu(t)\longrightarrow0.
\]
This criterion applies without change to absolutely continuous, singular,
and purely atomic laws.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proposition}[Thin-shell aggregation precedes the entropy nonlinearity]
\label{prop:pre-phi-thin-shell-aggregation}
Fix \(d\ge4\), and set
\[
 p=\frac{4(d+1)}{d+5},\qquad q=\frac{d+5}{d+1}.
\]
Let \(B_n\to\infty\), choose \(R_n\to\infty\), and put
\(a_n=B_nR_n^{-p}\to0\) and
\(t_n=\kappa R_na_n^{1/(d+1)}\), where \(\kappa>0\) is fixed and
sufficiently small.  Let \(K_n\) be integers such that
\[
 B_n^qK_n^{1-q}\longrightarrow0.
\]
There are atoms \(\eta_{n,j}=(a_n/K_n)\delta_{r_{n,j}e_1}\),
\(1\le j\le K_n\), with
\(R_n\le r_{n,j}\le R_n+t_n/10\), for which
\begin{align}
 \sum_{j=1}^{K_n}\sup_{t>0}t^4
 \int\Phi(V_t^{\eta_{n,j}})\dd\Omega_d&\longrightarrow0,
 \label{eq:thin-shell-separated-energy}\\
 t_n^4\int\Phi\!\left(\sum_{j=1}^{K_n}
 V_{t_n}^{\eta_{n,j}}\right)\dd\Omega_d&\longrightarrow+\infty.
 \label{eq:thin-shell-aggregated-energy}
\end{align}
Thus summing shellwise nonlinear energies cannot replace aggregation of the
positive tail potential before applying \(\Phi\).
Here \(V_t^\eta\) denotes the expression on the right side of
\eqref{eq:raw-tail-poisson-potential} with the finite measure \(\eta\) in
place of \(\nu_c\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{corollary}[Proxy characterization at the critical vague boundary]
\label{cor:critical-rv-proxy-characterization}
Fix \(d\ge4\), set
\[
 p_d=\frac{4(d+1)}{d+5},
 \qquad
 \mu_\sigma(\dd\rho,\dd\theta)
 =p_d\rho^{-p_d-1}\dd\rho\,\sigma(\dd\theta),
\]
and let \(\nu\) have mean \(\bar\gamma\), finite covariance \(\Sigma\), and
centred law \(\nu_c\).  Suppose that, for a slowly varying \(L\),
\[
 \frac{r^{p_d}}{L(r)}\nu_c(r\,\mathord\cdot)
 \xrightarrow[r\to\infty]{v}\mu_\sigma
 \quad\text{on }\RR^d\setminus\{0\},
 \qquad
 \ell_L(t):=\int_{R_0}^t\frac{L(s)}s\dd s\longrightarrow\infty.
\]
Then
\[
 t^4H_d(t)\longrightarrow\mathcal Q_d(\Sigma)
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \mathcal C_{\nu,\Sigma}(t)\longrightarrow0.
\]
In particular, for the two laws \(\nu_{\rm ac}\) and \(\nu_{\rm cl}\) in
Theorem~\ref{thm:critical-vague-tail-nonidentifiability},
\[
 \mathcal C_{\nu_{\rm ac},\Sigma}(t)\longrightarrow0,
 \qquad
 \limsup_{t\to\infty}\mathcal C_{\nu_{\rm cl},\Sigma}(t)=+\infty.
\]
Thus the spectral measure and slowly varying factor do not determine the
covariance-proxy defect trajectory.
\end{corollary}

For a formulation involving only masses of moving sets, define
\[
 A_{t,0}(y)=\{|x|>t:\ |x-ty|<t\},
\]
and, for \(k\ge1\),
\[
 A_{t,k}(y)=\{|x|>t:\ 2^{k-1}t\le|x-ty|<2^kt\}.
\]
Put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:moving-annulus-tail-functional}
 M_t^\nu(y)=(1+|y|^2)^a\left\{
 \nu_c(A_{t,0}(y))+
 \sum_{k\ge1}2^{-k(d+1)}\nu_c(A_{t,k}(y))\right\}
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:moving-annulus-tail-energy}
 G_\nu(t)=t^4\int_{\RR^d}\Phi(M_t^\nu)\dd\Omega_d.
\end{equation}

\begin{corollary}[Moving-annulus raw-tail criterion]
\label{cor:moving-annulus-tail-criterion}
Under the hypotheses of
Theorem~\ref{thm:raw-tail-poisson-energy-decomposition},
\[
 t^4H_d(t)\longrightarrow\mathcal Q_d(\Sigma)
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \mathcal C_{\nu,\Sigma}(t)\longrightarrow0
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 G_\nu(t)\longrightarrow0.
\]
This criterion applies without change to absolutely continuous, singular,
and purely atomic laws.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
On \(A_{t,0}(y)\), the factor
\((1+|y-x/t|^2)^{-a}\) lies between \(2^{-a}\) and \(1\).  On
\(A_{t,k}(y)\), \(k\ge1\), it is comparable, with constants depending only
on \(d\), to \(2^{-k(d+1)}\).  Hence
\[
 c_dM_t^\nu(y)\le V_t^\nu(y)\le C_dM_t^\nu(y).
\]
For each fixed \(c>0\), the ratio \(\Phi(cv)/\Phi(v)\) has positive finite
limits \(c^2\) at zero and \(c\) at infinity and is continuous on
\((0,\infty)\).  Thus fixed multiplicative changes of the argument preserve
two-sided comparability of \(\Phi\).  The conclusion follows from
\eqref{eq:raw-tail-proxy-equivalence}.
\end{proof}

\label{prop:pre-phi-thin-shell-aggregation}
